| 21st Annual Report 2025-26 Supreme Power Equipment Limited | 32
Transforming
Capacity into Growth
Capacity Created. Growth Unlocked
For most of our history, the question we asked ourselves was how much more we could make. This 
year, we answered it. The commissioning of our Kannur facility took our installed capacity from 2,500 
MVA to approximately 9,000 MVA and lifted the ceiling on what we can build from 50 MVA to 200 
MVA, and from 132 kV to 220 kV. It is the single largest investment in the Company’s history and the 
single largest expansion of what we are able to do. Capacity, however, is only potential. What makes 
it growth is the order book it attracts, the customers it qualifies us for and the projects it lets us bid. 
On all three counts, FY2025-26 has already begun to answer. We closed the year with an order book 
of Rs.588.17 Crore, a customer base that now spans state utilities, EPC contractors, public sector 
enterprises and renewable developers across four states, and a product range that reaches from last-
mile distribution to transmission-class power transformers. The capacity has been built. The growth 
has started.

| 21st Annual Report 2025-26 Supreme Power Equipment Limited |

Company Overview Financial StatementsStatutory Report
Engineering Reliability. 
Delivering Performance.  
Founded in 2005 and headquartered in Tamil Nadu, we have built over two decades of expertise in manufacturing 
power and distribution transformers for India’s growing power sector. We provide transformer solutions across 
power generation, transmission, distribution and renewable energy, serving utilities, EPC contractors and 
industrial customers across the country.
With an annual manufacturing capacity of approximately 9,000 MVA and the capability to manufacture 
transformers up to 200 MVA and 220 kV, we are well positioned to address larger and higher-value projects. Our 
diversified product portfolio is built on a strong foundation of engineering excellence, quality and reliability, 
enabling us to meet the evolving requirements of the power industry.
Backed by long-standing customer relationships, a growing order book and expanding manufacturing 
capabilities, we continue to strengthen our presence across India’s power infrastructure landscape. We remain 
focused on delivering innovative, reliable and efficient transformer solutions while creating sustainable value 
for all our stakeholders.
We have been listed on the NSE Emerge platform since December 29, 2023.

FY2025-26
Building Capacity. 
Winning Opportunities. 
Creating Scale.
FY2025-26 marked a significant step forward in our growth journey. Throughout the year, 
we strengthened our manufacturing capabilities, secured healthy order inflows, expanded 
into higher-capacity transformer solutions and deepened our presence across utilities, EPC 
companies, industrial customers and renewable energy projects. These achievements have 
enhanced our competitiveness, broadened our market opportunities and positioned us to 
create sustainable long-term value as India's power infrastructure continues to evolve. 
Positioned for the Next Phase of Growth
The successful commissioning of our new manufacturing facility marks an important step in our growth 
journey. Combined with healthy order momentum and our increasing participation in higher-capacity 
transformer projects, it has strengthened our ability to serve a wider range of customers and larger projects. 
Backed by expanded manufacturing capabilities, a diversified customer base and a growing presence across 
utility, industrial and renewable energy sectors, we are confident of creating sustainable long-term value 
while capitalising on the significant opportunities unfolding in India's power infrastructure landscape.

Transmission Distribution End User
Energy-efficient transformers minimise technical losses, 
reduce operating costs and improve system efficiency, 
supporting sustainable power infrastructure.
Power transformers enable efficient bulk power transmission 
across high-voltage networks. With capacities up to 200 MVA and 
220 kV, they deliver reliable, efficient and low-loss performance. 
Converter and rectifier transformers enable reliable AC-to-
DC power conversion for demanding industrial applications, 
ensuring continuous and efficient operation.
Isolation transformers provide electrical isolation, 
enhancing safety and power quality while protecting 
sensitive equipment from electrical disturbances.
Energy-efficient transformers minimise technical losses, 
reduce operating costs and improve system efficiency, 
supporting sustainable power infrastructure.
Windmill transformers efficiently transfer wind-generated 
power to the grid, ensuring reliable performance under 
variable loads and demanding conditions. 
Solar transformers enable efficient evacuation and grid 
integration of solar power, supporting stable and reliable 
performance across renewable energy projects.
Generator transformers transfer power from generation 
plants to transmission networks, ensuring efficient power 
evacuation and reliable system stability.
Transformer tanks and accessories provide structural 
strength, effective cooling and durability, ensuring safe 
operation and extended equipment life.

Trusted Across the

Engineered for Quality.
Built for Reliability.
Delivering dependable transformer solutions through stringent quality systems, certified 
processes and uncompromising engineering standards.
Trusted Across the Power Ecosystem
At Supreme Power Equipment Limited, quality is 
embedded across every stage of our manufacturing 
process from design validation and raw material 
inspection to precision manufacturing, testing 
and final dispatch. Every transformer undergoes 
rigorous stage-wise quality checks and 
comprehensive testing to ensure compliance with 
customer specifications and applicable industry 
standards.
Our manufacturing processes are supported by 
advanced in-house testing facilities and a robust 
quality management framework, enabling us 
to consistently deliver transformers that meet 
the highest standards of safety, performance 
and operational reliability. A testament to our 
engineering excellence is our track record: none of 
the transformers supplied by the Company have 
experienced a field failure to date, reflecting our 
unwavering commitment to quality and customer 
trust.
Our commitment to continuous improvement, 
technological advancement and process excellence 
enables us to deliver transformer solutions that 
perform reliably across critical power infrastructure 
applications throughout their operating life.

Expanding Capacity.
Elevating Capabilities.
The commencement of our new manufacturing facility marks a significant 
step in our journey, strengthening our ability to serve larger, higher-value 
opportunities across India’s evolving power infrastructure.
End-to-End In-House Manufacturing Process
Designed for Tomorrow’s 
Power Infrastructure
The commercialisation of our new manufacturing facility at 
Kannur, Tamil Nadu, marks a defining milestone in the Company's 
manufacturing journey. Built with an investment of approximately 
Rs.100 Crore, the facility substantially expands our production 
capabilities while enabling entry into the high-capacity, high-voltage 
transformer segment.
With the ability to manufacture transformers up to 200 MVA and 220 kV, 
the new facility broadens our addressable market beyond conventional 
distribution applications, allowing us to participate in transmission 
infrastructure, utility projects, industrial developments and other 
technically demanding applications.
Our manufacturing operations are now supported by two facilities 
in Tamil Nadu that together provide the flexibility to serve a wider 
spectrum of customer requirements.
The Thirumazhisai facility continues to manufacture power and 
distribution transformers up to 25 MVA / 132 kV, while the Kannur 
facility significantly enhances our capabilities with transformer 
manufacturing up to 200 MVA / 220 kV.
Together, these facilities provide a combined installed manufacturing 
capacity of approximately 9,000 MVA per annum, positioning us to 
support the next phase of the Company’s growth.
Every transformer is manufactured through a 
closely coordinated production process supported 
by specialised equipment, in-house testing 
and stringent quality controls. This integrated 
manufacturing approach enables consistent 
product quality, efficient execution and reliable 
delivery across a broad range of transformer 
solutions.
The new manufacturing facility significantly enhances 
our ability to address the evolving requirements of 
India's power sector. Its expanded capabilities enable 
us to participate in larger and more complex projects 
while supporting applications across transmission 
infrastructure, renewable energy, industrial facilities 
and data centres.
The facility also provides greater manufacturing 
flexibility, enabling faster execution, improved 
production scalability and the ability to manufacture a 
broader portfolio of transformer solutions.

CSR Activities
At Supreme Power Equipment Limited, corporate responsibility is an extension of our 
commitment to sustainable and inclusive progress. We seek to contribute meaningfully to 
society by supporting initiatives that address essential social needs and improve quality of 
life.
Our CSR efforts are directed towards healthcare, women empowerment, senior citizen welfare 
and education, with an emphasis on initiatives that create practical, long-term benefits for 
individuals and communities.
Our Focus Areas
Healthcare & Sanitation
Supporting preventive 
healthcare, sanitation and 
initiatives that contribute to 
healthier communities.
Women Empowerment
Promoting greater self-reliance, 
opportunity and economic 
participation among women.
Senior Citizen Welfare
Supporting facilities and 
initiatives that provide care, 
comfort and dignity to senior 
citizens.
Education
Encouraging access to learning 
and special education to 
help individuals realise their 
potential
Enabling Inclusion. 
Creating Opportunity.
An important part of Supreme Power’s 
CSR approach is supporting intellectually 
disabled and differently abled children  
through special education, care, skill 
development and inclusive opportunities.
The objective is to create an environment 
where individuals are encouraged to learn, 
develop capabilities and participate more 
confidently in society. By supporting such 
initiatives, the Company aims to contribute 
towards greater inclusion while enabling 
beneficiaries to build a stronger sense of 
independence and dignity.
Our CSR philosophy is centred on purposeful 
action, where every initiative seeks to create 
measurable value beyond the organisation. 
As Supreme Power continues to grow, 
we remain committed to strengthening 
communities and contributing to a more 
inclusive future.
Beyond Power, Towards Progress
Our responsibility extends beyond 
powering infrastructure to helping 
create stronger communities and better 
opportunities for people.

Board of Directors
Confident leadership, purposeful direction
VEE RAJMOHAN 
Chairman & Managing Director | 30+ Years of Experience
VISHWAMBRAN NAIR PRADEEP KUMAR 
Whole Time Director
Mr. Vee Rajmohan is the Founder of Supreme Power Equipment 
Limited and has been instrumental in transforming the Company 
from a partnership firm into an established transformer 
manufacturer. With over three decades of entrepreneurial and 
industry experience, he has led the Company’s strategic growth, 
manufacturing expansion and market development.
Prior to establishing the Company, he gained extensive experience 
in industrial plant operations, including machinery installation, 
commissioning and commercialisation. He continues to provide 
strategic leadership across business development, operations and 
long-term growth initiatives.
Mr. Devaraja Iyer Krishna Iyer brings over four decades of experience 
in transformer engineering and power systems. His expertise spans 
transformer design, manufacturing, plant operations and high-voltage 
technologies, providing valuable technical guidance to the Board.
He has held leadership positions with organisations including 
TELK, AREVA, ECE Transformers and Prime Meiden, and has received 
specialised technical training from Hitachi, Japan and AREVA, Germany.
Mr. Perumal Ravikumar has over three decades of experience in human 
resources, organisational development and talent management. His 
professional journey includes leadership roles across India and the 
Asia-Pacific region, with expertise in people strategy, administration 
and organisational effectiveness.
His extensive experience in managing large and diverse workforces 
brings valuable perspectives on leadership, governance and 
organisational development.
Ms. Saimathy Soupramanien is a Practising Company Secretary with 
over 26 years of experience in corporate governance, secretarial practices 
and regulatory compliance. She possesses extensive knowledge of 
corporate laws, legal frameworks and governance standards.
Her multidisciplinary expertise across legal, finance, accounting 
and taxation strengthens the Company’s governance framework and 
supports adherence to evolving regulatory requirements.
Mr. K. V. Pradeep Kumar has been associated with the Company 
since its inception and brings over three decades of expertise in 
transformer design, manufacturing, testing and quality assurance. 
He has played a significant role in strengthening the Company’s 
technical capabilities and manufacturing excellence.
Before joining the Company, he held senior technical positions 
at Indotech Transformers (PROLEC GE), where he was responsible 
for transformers up to 100 MVA / 230 kV. At SPEL, he oversees 
manufacturing, quality, procurement and technical operations.

Company Overview Financial StatementsStatutory Report
Chairman & Managing 
       Director’s Letter 
Dear Shareholders,
It gives me great pleasure to address you at the close 
of what has been a significant and transformative 
year for Supreme Power Equipment Limited.
The Company was incorporated in 2005 in Tamil Nadu, 
marking the beginning of its journey as a corporate 
entity. Since its incorporation, the Company has 
steadily expanded its operations and strengthened 
its presence in the industry. Our objective was clear 
and straightforward — to manufacture reliable 
transformers that our customers could depend upon. 
More than Two decades later, that commitment to 
quality and reliability continues to define us. I am 
proud to state that none of the transformers supplied 
by the Company has recorded a field failure to date.
FY2025-26 represents an important milestone in our 
journey. During the year, we significantly expanded our 
manufacturing capabilities, strengthened our order 
book and positioned the Company to participate in 
larger and more complex opportunities across India’s 
power transmission and distribution sectors.
A Transformational Expansion
One of the most important achievements of the year 
was the commissioning of our new manufacturing 
facility at Kannur, Tamil Nadu, involving an investment 
of approximately Rs.100 Crore — the largest capital 
investment undertaken by the Company to date.
With the commissioning of this facility, our installed 
manufacturing capacity has increased from 2,500 
MVA to approximately 9,000 MVA per annum. More 
importantly, our product capability has expanded from 
transformers of up to 50 MVA and 132 kV to transformers 
of up to 200 MVA and 220 kV.
This represents much more than an increase in 
capacity. Entry into the 220 kV class enables us to 
address a significantly larger market and participate 
in major transmission and infrastructure projects 
that were previously beyond our manufacturing range.
Our Thirumazhisai facility will continue to serve 
the distribution and mid-range power transformer 
segments up to 25 MVA and 132 kV. Together, our 
Thirumazhisai and Kannur facilities provide us with a 
broader manufacturing platform and greater flexibility 
to execute both large and small orders simultaneously.
The expansion has therefore transformed SPEL from 
a predominantly distribution-focused transformer 
manufacturer into a Company capable of serving both 
the distribution and transmission segments of India’s 
power sector.
Financial Performance
The Company delivered healthy financial growth 
during FY2025-26.
On a consolidated basis, revenue increased by 22.1% 
to Rs.181.6 Crore, while total income grew by 21.78% to 
Rs.182.1 Crore. EBITDA increased by 14.5% to Rs.33.3 
Crore, and Profit Before Tax stood at Rs.28.5 Crore. Net 
Profit increased to Rs.20.4 Crore from Rs.18.6 Crore in 
the previous year, while Earnings Per Share improved 
from Rs.7.4 to Rs.8.2.
Our net worth strengthened significantly, increasing 
from Rs.92.5 Crore to Rs.112.9 Crore, supported entirely 
by retained earnings.
While profitability grew at a slower pace than 
revenue during the year, this should be viewed in the 
context of our expansion. Depreciation and finance 
costs increased following the capitalisation of the 
new facility and the borrowings associated with 
the expansion. Employee and operating costs also 
increased as we built the team required to operate our 
second manufacturing facility.
In effect, the Company incurred the costs associated 
with building future capacity while receiving only 
a partial contribution from the additional capacity 
during the year. As utilisation at Kannur increases, we 
expect the operating benefits of this investment to 
become increasingly visible.
At the same time, we are pleased that the largest 
capital expenditure programme in the Company’s 
history was undertaken while maintaining positive 
net cash flow and a conservative gearing level of 0.42 
times.
Our working capital management also improved 
during the year, with receivable days reducing to 94 
despite revenue increasing by more than 20%. Given 
the extended payment cycles that are common in 
the power sector, particularly with state utilities, this 
represents an important operational achievement.
“Capacity by itself represents potential. 
Sustainable growth comes from converting 
that capacity into orders, execution and 
customer confidence. We have created the 
capacity; our focus now is to convert it into 
long-term growth.”
Strong Order Book and Changing Business Mix
Order inflow remained healthy throughout the year 
and was well distributed across customer segments.
During FY2025-26, the Company secured orders 
exceeding Rs.437 Crore. These included orders 
aggregating Rs.113.61 Crore from Kerala State Electricity 
Board, TNPDCL and Danya Electric Company; an order 
of Rs.60.90 Crore from a Navratna Public Sector 
Enterprise for inverter duty transformers; multiple 
high-value orders from EPC companies in Karnataka 
and Telangana; and continued enquiries from 
renewable energy developers requiring specialised 
transformer solutions.
As a result, our consolidated order book stood at 
Rs.588.17 Crore as on May 27, 2026 — more than three 
times the revenue recorded during FY2025-26.
More important than the size of the order book is the 
change in its composition. Approximately 72% of the 
current order book comprises power transformers, 
around 20% comprises distribution transformers, with 
the balance consisting of inverter duty transformers.
This shift reflects the changing profile of SPEL and 
demonstrates that our investment in expanded 
manufacturing capabilities is already enabling us to 
participate in larger and higher-value opportunities.
As we expand our 
capacity, deepen our 
private sector presence, 
and align with India’s 
clean energy goals, 
we remain focused on 
building long-term value 
through reliable execution 
and steady growth.

A Strong Industry Opportunity
The outlook for India’s power sector remains highly 
encouraging.
The National Electricity Plan (Transmission) released 
by the Ministry of Power envisages investments 
exceeding Rs.9.15 lakh Crore in the transmission 
sector up to 2032 and a network capable of supporting 
more than 600 GW of renewable energy capacity.
The continued strengthening of India’s transmission 
and distribution infrastructure creates substantial 
long-term opportunities for transformer 
manufacturers.
At the distribution level, the Revamped Distribution 
Sector Scheme continues to support investments by 
state utilities in strengthening and modernising their 
networks.
At the same time, new areas of electricity demand 
are emerging rapidly. Data centres, electric vehicle 
charging infrastructure, railway electrification, 
renewable energy projects and green hydrogen 
are creating new requirements for reliable power 
infrastructure.
Transformers are an essential component across this 
entire electricity value chain — from generation and 
transmission to distribution and final consumption. 
With our expanded manufacturing capabilities, we 
believe SPEL is well positioned to participate in these 
opportunities.
Managing Challenges with Discipline
While the opportunity is significant, we remain 
conscious of the challenges.
Cold-rolled grain-oriented electrical steel (CRGO), a 
critical raw material in transformer manufacturing, 
continues to be substantially import-dependent. Its 
availability and pricing therefore remain important 
external variables for our business.
Competition in the higher-rating transformer 
segment is also strong, particularly from established 
manufacturers with longer operating histories in the 
220 kV category.
We recognise that capability in this segment cannot 
be established through manufacturing capacity 
alone. Customer confidence must be earned through 
successful type testing, qualification, first-article 
approvals, timely execution and consistent product 
performance.
We will therefore continue to approach our expansion 
into higher voltage classes in a disciplined and 
measured manner.
Recognition, Governance and Financial Standing
During the year, CRISIL upgraded the Company’s long-
term credit rating to BBB/Stable and its short-term 
rating to A3+.
The improved rating strengthens our financial 
standing and is particularly important in our industry, 
where working capital facilities and bank guarantee 
limits directly influence the scale of projects and 
tenders in which we can participate.
We were also honoured to receive the MSME Ratna 
Award 2025.
The Company continues to maintain ISO 9001:2015, 
ISO 14001:2015 and ISO 45001:2018 certifications and 
remains empanelled with major utilities including 
TANGEDCO, KSEB, KPTCL and TNPDCL.
As our operations have expanded to a second 
manufacturing location, we have correspondingly 
strengthened our governance and internal control 
framework, including inventory management, 
plant-level authorisation controls and fixed asset 
management.
I would also like to acknowledge the valuable guidance 
and support of my fellow Board members, whose 
experience across transformer engineering, corporate 
governance and organisational development has been 
particularly valuable during this important phase of 
expansion.
Our People – The Foundation of Our Growth
Our achievements during the year were made possible 
not by capital investment alone, but by the dedication 
and commitment of our people.
Commissioning a new manufacturing facility while 
simultaneously executing a growing order book 
required considerable effort across the organisation. 
Our teams at Thirumazhisai and Kannur rose to this 
challenge with commitment and professionalism.
During the year, we strengthened our teams across 
design, production, testing and quality functions. 
We will continue to invest in training and skill 
development, particularly in specialised areas such 
as coil winding, core assembly, tanking and testing, 
where technical expertise and workmanship directly 
influence product quality.
As the transformer industry expands, the demand for 
experienced technical professionals is increasing. 
Attracting, developing and retaining skilled people 
will therefore remain an important strategic priority 
for SPEL.
Looking Ahead
We enter FY2026-27 from a position of strength, with 
an order book of Rs.588.17 Crore and a substantially 
expanded manufacturing base.
Our primary focus will be on progressively increasing 
utilisation at the Kannur facility, which we expect to 
approach optimal utilisation over a two-to-three-year 
period.
Our priorities for the coming year will be to increase 
production at Kannur, secure qualifications and 
approvals in higher voltage classes, deepen our 
relationships with utilities and EPC customers, expand 
our geographical presence, strengthen critical raw 
material sourcing and maintain disciplined working 
capital management.
Above all, we will continue to protect the quality and 
reliability standards on which SPEL has built its 
reputation.
Building the Next Phase of SPEL
FY2025-26 has been a year of building for the future.
The manufacturing capacity is now in place. Our 
order book is the largest in the Company’s history. 
Our balance sheet remains healthy, and the market 
opportunity before us is substantial.
The next phase of our journey will therefore be 
defined by execution — converting capacity into 
production, production into revenues and revenues 
into sustainable returns for our shareholders.
I am confident that our organisation has the technical 
capabilities, manufacturing infrastructure, customer 
relationships and experienced team required to 
achieve this objective.

The investments we have made represent our 
commitment to the future of the Company. We 
remain focused on converting these investments into 
sustainable growth, stronger profitability and long-

| 21st Annual Report 2025-26 Supreme Power Equipment Limited |

Management 
   Discussion and Analysis
1. Company Overview
Supreme Power Equipment Limited is a Tamil Nadu-headquartered manufacturer of power and distribution 
transformers, serving India’s generation, transmission, distribution and renewable energy sectors. The 
Company was incorporated as Supreme Power Equipment Private Limited in 2005, and was listed on the NSE 
Emerge platform on 29 December 2023. Over more than three decades it has supplied in excess of 20,800 
transformer units to more than 880 customers across over 35 end-use sectors, and manufactures nine classes 
of transformers - power, distribution, energy efficient, generator, windmill, solar, inverter duty, converter and 
rectifier, and isolation transformers - together with transformer tanks and accessories. Manufacturing is carried 
out at two facilities in Tamil Nadu: the Thirumazhisai plant, which produces power and distribution transformers 
of up to 25 MVA and 132 kV, and the Kannur plant commissioned during the year under review, which extends 
capability to 200 MVA and 220 kV. Together the two facilities provide an installed capacity of approximately 
9,000 MVA per annum. The Company holds ISO 9001:2015, ISO 14001:2015 and ISO 45001:2018 certifications, is 
empanelled with state utilities including TANGEDCO, KSEB, KPTCL and TNPDCL, and to date has not recorded 
a field failure on any transformer it has supplied. As reported in the Company’s audited financial results for 
FY2025-26, which were approved by the Board on 27 May, 2026 and filed with the National Stock Exchange, 
consolidated results include Danya Electric Company, a partnership firm in which the Company holds a 90% 
interest.
The year in brief
FY2025-26 was defined by the commissioning of the Kannur facility, which raised installed capacity from 2,500 
MVA to approximately 9,000 MVA and lifted manufacturing capability from 50 MVA / 132 kV to 200 MVA / 220 kV. 
As per the audited FY2025-26 financial results filed with the National Stock Exchange, consolidated total income 
grew 21.78% to Rs.182.10 Crore, EBITDA grew 14.52% to Rs.33.29 Crore and profit after tax grew 9.89% to Rs.20.44 
Crore. As disclosed at the H2 and FY2025-26 earnings conference call held in June 2026, the consolidated order 
book stood at Rs.588.17 Crore as on May 27, 2026.
2. Economic Overview
2.1 Global economy
The global economy entered FY2025-26 in a more settled position than had been feared at the start of the 
year, with the International Monetary Fund, in its World Economic Outlook update, projecting world output 
growth of around 3.0% in 2025 and 3.1% in 2026 - an upward revision reflecting lower effective tariff rates than 
initially assumed, improved financial conditions and fiscal expansion in several major economies. Growth 
nevertheless remains below the pre-pandemic average, and the composition of risk has shifted rather than 
diminished. Trade policy uncertainty, geopolitical friction in West Asia, volatility in crude oil prices and the 
pace at which major central banks unwind restrictive policy continue to shape the outlook. For capital goods 
manufacturers, the more consequential global development has been the tightening of supply in Specialised 
electrical materials and equipment as grid investment accelerates simultaneously across the United States, 
Europe, the Middle East and Asia. Lead times for transformers, high-voltage equipment and grain-oriented 
electrical steel have lengthened worldwide, which supports pricing but places a premium on procurement 
discipline.
2.2 Indian economy
India remained the fastest-growing major economy through the year. According to the First Advance Estimates 
released by the National Statistics Office, Ministry of Statistics and Programme Implementation, in January 
2026, real GDP growth for FY2025-26 stood at 7.4%, ahead of the 6.3-6.8% range projected at the start of 
the year and above the 6.5% recorded in FY2024-25, with subsequent revisions placing growth marginally 
higher still. Growth was supported by resilient private consumption, a continued expansion in gross fixed 
capital formation and sustained public capital expenditure. As per the Reserve Bank of India’s Monetary 
Policy Statement of June 2026, real GDP growth of 6.6% is projected for FY2026-27 with CPI inflation of 5.1%, 
the policy repo rate having been held at 5.25% with a neutral stance; the same review placed manufacturing 
capacity utilisation at 75.2% in the fourth quarter of FY2025-26, above its long-term average of about 74%. 
The moderation projected for FY2026-27 reflects base effects and external volatility rather than domestic 
weakness.
For the Company, the relevant feature of the Indian macro picture is not the headline growth rate but its 
composition. Public and quasi-public capital expenditure on infrastructure - and within it, power infrastructure 
- has been the most consistent element of Indian demand for several years, and is underwritten by multi-year 
central schemes rather than by annual budget cycles. That gives equipment manufacturers a visibility that 
is unusual in the broader capital goods sector.
3. Industry Structure and Developments
3.1 India’s power sector
According to the Centre for Research on Energy and Clean Air, in its April 2026 review of India’s power sector for 
the fourth quarter of FY2025-26, the country’s installed generation capacity reached approximately 533 GW by 
March 2026, with non-fossil sources accounting for around 53% of the total - a threshold crossed well ahead 
of the original commitment date. Capacity addition in FY2025-26 was the highest in the country’s history: on 
Central Electricity Authority data, 52,537 MW was added up to 31 January 2026, of which approximately 43 GW 
came from renewable sources, taking total non-fossil capacity to about 272 GW. As per the Central Electricity 
Authority’s Long-Term National Resource Adequacy Plan (2026-27 to 2035-36) of March 2026, read with the 
CREA review referred to above, solar accounts for the largest share of the renewable build-out at roughly 140-
150 GW, followed by wind at about 55 GW and hydro at about 51 GW.
Demand has grown at least as quickly as supply. As recorded in the same Long-Term National Resource 
Adequacy Plan, peak electricity demand rose from 190 GW in FY2020-21 to 250 GW in FY2024-25, a compound 
annual growth rate of 7.06% and well above the long-term historical average of 4-5%. The Central Electricity 
Authority’s National Generation Adequacy Plan for 2026-27 to 2035-36, released in March 2026 alongside 
the midterm review of the 20th Electric Power Survey, projects peak demand rising to 459 GW and electricity 
requirement to 3,365 BU by 2035-36, with installed capacity more than doubling from about 520 GW in 
January 2026 to 1,121 GW over the same period. A structural shift in the shape of demand is also underway, 
with peak load increasingly occurring during solar generation hours rather than in the evening, which raises 
the importance of grid flexibility, storage and transformation capacity at the distribution end.
3.2 Transmission: the defining opportunity
As announced by the Ministry of Power, Government of India, the National Electricity Plan (Transmission) 
released in October 2024 sets out an investment opportunity of over Rs.9,15,000 Crore in the transmission 
sector up to 2032. Per the Ministry, the plan is designed to build a network capable of supporting 500 GW 
of non-fossil capacity by 2030 and over 600 GW by 2032. In physical terms the Plan, prepared by the Central 
Electricity Authority, envisages the addition of approximately 1,91,000 circuit kilometres of transmission lines 
and more than 1,274 GVA of transformation capacity by FY2032, expanding the national network from roughly 
5 lakh circuit kilometres in early 2026 to about 6.48 lakh circuit kilometres. On an analysis of the Plan data 
for the 2027-32 period published by InVed in July 2026, the substantial majority of the investment identified 
for that period is directed at the Inter-State Transmission System, with the balance at intra-state networks.
Execution is already visible in procurement. According to a Kotak Neo research report of May 2026 on India’s 
transmission capital expenditure cycle, Power Grid Corporation of India recorded capital expenditure of 
Rs.35,540 Crore in FY2025-26, exceeding its revised target, and has planned capital expenditure of Rs.1.08 
[page 17]
| 21st Annual Report 2025-26 Supreme Power Equipment Limited |
3130
Company Overview Financial StatementsStatutory Report 
lakh Crore between FY2026 and FY2028 against an estimated business pipeline of Rs.3.06 lakh Crore through 
FY2032. State transmission utilities are running parallel programmes. The significance for transformer 
manufacturers is that transformation capacity - measured in MVA and GVA - is the single largest equipment 
line item in a transmission build-out, and the plan’s 1,274 GVA target represents demand of a different order 
from anything the industry has previously absorbed.
3.3 Distribution: reform-linked demand
On the distribution side, as set out by the Ministry of Power, Government of India, the Revamped Distribution 
Sector Scheme launched in July 2021 carries a total outlay of Rs.3,03,758 Crore, including gross budgetary 
support of Rs.97,631 Crore, over the five years from FY2021-22 to FY2025-26, with the objectives of reducing 
aggregate technical and commercial losses to a pan-India range of 12-15% and closing the gap between 
average cost of supply and average revenue realised. Because the scheme is reforms-linked and results-
linked, and because distribution companies procure distribution transformers directly as part of network 
strengthening, it creates creditworthy and repeatable demand for manufacturers empanelled with state 
utilities. Separately, the Bureau of Energy Efficiency’s upgraded star-labelling requirements for distribution 
transformers, effective from 1 January 2025, have raised the efficiency threshold across the segment and, on 
the Global Trade Research Initiative’s assessment, pushed the industry towards higher-grade core material 
and better design.
3.4 Renewable energy and evacuation infrastructure
India’s renewable programme is now large enough that grid connection, rather than generation, is the binding 
constraint. Each gigawatt of solar or wind capacity requires inverter duty, solar or windmill transformers at 
the generation end, step-up transformers at the pooling substation and reinforced transformation capacity 
along the evacuation corridor. The record renewable addition of approximately 43 GW in FY2025-26 reported by 
the Central Electricity Authority therefore translates, on its own, into a substantial and recurring transformer 
requirement. The move towards hybrid and storage-linked projects, and towards green energy open access 
for commercial and industrial consumers, adds further specialised transformer demand.
3.5 New categories of demand
A set of load categories that were immaterial a decade ago are now shaping equipment procurement. As per 
CBRE Group’s India data centre report for 2026, the country’s data centre stock crossed approximately 1.7 
GW in 2025 following a supply addition of 440 MW, and total capacity across major cities is expected to rise 
by about 30% during 2026 on roughly 500 MW of fresh supply, with cumulative investment commitments 
in the sector reaching about USD 126 billion by the end of 2025. Looking further out, Wood Mackenzie, in its 
India data centre market outlook of July 2026, places operational capacity at 12 GW by 2030. Data centres 
require high reliability, high-quality power and dedicated transformation infrastructure, and Tamil Nadu, 
Telangana, Maharashtra, Andhra Pradesh and Uttar Pradesh are expected to lead investment - markets in 
which the Company already has an established presence. Railway electrification, electric vehicle charging 
infrastructure, green hydrogen electrolysers and industrial corridor development add further, and largely 
uncorrelated, demand.
3.6 The Indian transformer industry
Against this backdrop, Mordor Intelligence estimates the Indian transformer market at approximately USD 
3.25 billion in 2026 and forecasts it to reach about USD 4.82 billion by 2031 at a compound annual growth rate 
of roughly 8.2%; on the same estimates, distribution transformers accounted for about 59% of the market in 
2025 and power utilities for about 47% of revenue, with the commercial segment growing fastest. The industry 
is fragmented, with a small group of large multinational and domestic manufacturers competing in the 
extra-high-voltage segment and a long tail of regional manufacturers in distribution. The principal structural 
barrier to entry in the higher-rated segments is not capital but qualification: utility vendor approvals, type-
test certification at accredited laboratories and a demonstrated field track record typically take years to 
establish. This is where the Company’s zero-field-failure record, its existing utility empanelments and its 
investment in in-house testing infrastructure carry disproportionate value.
The most significant industry-level constraint remains raw material. Cold-rolled grain-oriented electrical 
steel, the core material for transformers, is substantially imported: domestic production has historically met 
only around 10-12% of Indian demand, and the Global Trade Research Initiative has assessed the shortfall at 
close to 30%. Domestic capacity is being built - on capacity announcements reported in the trade press, JSW 
JFE Electrical Steel is targeting 350,000 tonnes per annum by FY2028 - but the transition to self-reliance will 
take several years, and in the interim availability, Bureau of Indian Standards licensing timelines for foreign 
suppliers, and price volatility in CRGO steel, copper and transformer oil remain the principal cost risks for 
the sector.
4. Opportunities and Threats
Opportunities
 » A committed national transmission investment programme of over Rs.9.15 lakh Crore to 2032 under the 
Ministry of Power’s National Electricity Plan (Transmission), of which transformation capacity is a core 
component, provides a demand runway that extends well beyond a normal capital goods cycle.
 » The Company’s new capability of up to 200 MVA and 220 kV opens an addressable market - transmission-
class utility and industrial projects - that was previously closed to it, at a point when that market is 
expanding fastest.
 » Renewable evacuation requirements create recurring demand for inverter duty, solar and windmill 
transformers, a segment in which the Company already has supply credentials with leading developers.
 » Data centres and other mission-critical industrial loads represent a new, high-specification customer 
class which, on CBRE Group’s assessment, is concentrated in states where the Company is already 
established.
 » Distribution-side demand under the Ministry of Power’s Revamped Distribution Sector Scheme and 
under utility replacement cycles remains steady and reform-backed, providing a base load of orders that 
balances the lumpiness of large power transformer contracts.
 » Upgraded credit ratings improve access to working capital and bank guarantee limits, which is a practical 
constraint on bidding capacity for larger tenders.
Threats and challenges
 » Availability and pricing of CRGO electrical steel, copper and transformer oil, given the continued import 
dependence assessed by the Global Trade Research Initiative and the concentration of approved suppliers.
 » Competitive intensity in the higher-rated segment, where established multinational and large domestic 
manufacturers hold long-standing utility approvals and greater balance sheet depth.
 » Execution and qualification risk in the 200 MVA class, where type testing, first-article approvals and 
customer confidence must be built order by order.
 » Working capital intensity: transformer manufacturing carries long receivable cycles, particularly with 
state utilities, and larger orders lengthen both inventory holding and execution timelines.
 » Tender-driven pricing pressure and liquidated damages exposure on delayed execution.
 » Concentration of demand in a small number of large customers and in the southern region, notwithstanding 
the geographic widening achieved during the year.
 » Sector-level ordering can be uneven year to year even where the multi-year pipeline is intact since, as 
Kotak Neo has observed, right-of-way, land acquisition and clearance timelines shift award schedules.

5. Business Review for FY2025-26
5.1 Commissioning of the Kannur facility
The defining operational event of the year was the commencement of commercial production at the 
Company’s new manufacturing facility at Kannur, Tamil Nadu, built at an investment of approximately 
Rs.100 Crore. The facility extends manufacturing capability to transformers of up to 200 MVA and 220 kV and, 
together with the existing Thirumazhisai plant, takes combined installed capacity to approximately 9,000 
MVA per annum from 2,500 MVA previously. The Thirumazhisai facility continues to manufacture power and 
distribution transformers of up to 25 MVA and 132 kV, allowing the two plants to serve different segments of 
the order book without competing for the same production slots.
Particulars Earlier Current
Annual installed capacity 2,500 MVA Approximately 9,000 MVA
Transformer rating Up to 50 MVA Up to 200 MVA
Voltage class Up to 132 kV Up to 220 kV
Primary market Distribution Distribution and transmission
Addressable projects Small and mid-sized Large infrastructure projects
At the H2 and FY2025-26 earnings conference call held in June 2026, management indicated that the new 
facility is expected to reach optimal utilisation of approximately 90% over a two to three year period, and that 
peak revenue potential from the combined installed base is of the order of Rs.550 Crore. The commissioning 
also broadens the Company’s participation in transmission infrastructure, utility projects, industrial 
developments and data centre applications.
5.2 Order inflow and order book
Order inflow during FY2025-26 was healthy and broadly distributed through the year, with orders of over 
Rs.437 Crore secured across customer segments. Notable wins included orders aggregating Rs.113.61 Crore 
from Kerala State Electricity Board, TNPDCL and Danya Electric Company; an order of Rs.60.90 Crore from 
a Navratna public sector enterprise for inverter duty transformers; multiple high-value transformer orders 
from EPC companies in Karnataka and Telangana; and continued inflows from renewable energy projects 
requiring specialised transformer solutions.
The consolidated order book stood at Rs.588.17 Crore as on 27 May, 2026, comprising approximately 72% 
power transformers, 20% distribution transformers and 7-8% inverter duty transformers - a composition that 
reflects the shift in the Company’s product mix following the capacity expansion. Order momentum has 
continued into the current financial year, with further transformer orders from EPC and utility customers 
in Karnataka and Telangana announced through disclosures filed with the National Stock Exchange under 
Regulation 30 of the SEBI (LODR) Regulations, 2015 between April and July 2026.
5.3 Markets and customers
The Company serves five broad customer categories: state electricity utilities, EPC contractors, renewable 
energy developers, industrial manufacturing companies and public sector undertakings. During the year 
it deepened relationships across all five and widened its geographic footprint beyond Tamil Nadu into 
Kerala, Karnataka and Telangana. State empanelments with TANGEDCO, KSEB, KPTCL and TNPDCL provide 
direct access to utility tenders, while EPC relationships give access to project-linked demand where speed of 
delivery and technical flexibility matter more than lowest price.
5.4 Quality, certification and recognition
Quality systems remain the foundation of the Company’s customer proposition. Every transformer passes 
through stage-wise quality checks from design validation and raw material inspection through to routine 
and type testing before dispatch, supported by in-house testing facilities. The Company holds ISO 9001:2015, 
ISO 14001:2015 and ISO 45001:2018 certification. No power transformer supplied by the Company has recorded 
a field failure to date. During the year, on March 20th, 2026, CRISIL upgraded the Company’s long-term rating 
to BBB/Stable and its short-term rating to A3+, and the Company was a recipient of the MSME Ratna Award 
2025.
6. Product-wise Performance
The Company operates in a single reportable business segment - the manufacture and supply of transformers 
and related equipment - and accordingly segment reporting under Ind AS 108 is presented on that basis. 
Within that single segment the product portfolio spans nine transformer classes, and cumulative units 
supplied to date are set out below.

The order book composition disclosed at the FY2025-26 earnings conference call - approximately 72% power 
transformers against 20% distribution transformers - indicates that the revenue mix will shift materially 
towards higher-rated equipment over the coming two years.
7. Financial Performance
7.1 Statement of profit and loss
Consolidated revenue for FY2025-26 grew 22.1% to Rs.181.6 Crore from Rs.148.7 Crore, and total income grew 
21.78% to Rs.182.1 Crore. EBITDA increased 14.5% to Rs.33.3 Crore and profit before tax rose to Rs.28.5 Crore from 
Rs.26.1 Crore. Reported net profit after minority interest was Rs.20.4 Crore against Rs.18.6 Crore, and earnings 
per share improved to Rs.8.2 from Rs.7.4.
Profitability grew more slowly than revenue, and the reasons are structural rather than operational. 
Depreciation rose sharply to Rs.1.7 Crore from Rs.0.5 Crore, and finance costs to Rs.3.0 Crore from Rs.2.5 Crore, 
in both cases as a direct consequence of the new facility being capitalised and the borrowings raised to fund 
it. Employee costs increased to Rs.4.5 Crore from Rs.3.3 Crore as the Company recruited for the second plant, 
and other expenses rose to Rs.8.9 Crore from Rs.5.4 Crore. Raw material cost as a proportion of revenue was 
broadly stable. In effect, FY2025-26 carried a full year of expansion cost against a partial year of expansion 
revenue, and the margin compression this produced should be read in that light.

7.2 Balance sheet
Net worth grew to Rs.112.9 Crore from Rs.92.5 Crore, entirely through retained earnings, with reserves rising 
to Rs.87.9 Crore from Rs.67.5 Crore on an unchanged equity base of Rs.25.0 Crore. Total assets expanded to 
Rs.261.4 Crore from Rs.157.8 Crore, an increase of 65.7%, driven principally by fixed assets which rose to Rs.119.7 
Crore from Rs.64.0 Crore as the Kannur facility was capitalised.
Funding for the expansion came from a combination of internal accruals and borrowings. Non-current 
borrowings increased to Rs.33.1 Crore from Rs.9.1 Crore and current borrowings to Rs.16.9 Crore from Rs.9.7 
Crore, taking total debt to Rs.50.0 Crore and the debt-equity ratio to 0.42 times from 0.20 times. This remains 
a conservative level of gearing for a manufacturer that has just completed a capacity expansion of this 
magnitude. Inventories rose to Rs.62.1 Crore from Rs.31.5 Crore, reflecting both the larger order book and the 
longer material cycle associated with higher-rated transformers, while trade payables increased to Rs.62.2 
Crore from Rs.31.5 Crore. Trade receivables were broadly stable at Rs.46.8 Crore against Rs.45.2 Crore despite 
revenue growth of 22%, and, as disclosed at the FY2025-26 earnings conference call, receivable days improved 
to 94 days - a meaningful operational achievement in a sector where collection cycles with state utilities are 
typically extended.
7.3 Cash flow
Cash generated from operations was Rs.25.9 Crore against Rs.37.68 Crore in FY2024-25, the reduction 
reflecting the working capital absorbed by higher inventory levels. Cash used in investing activities rose to 
Rs.57.1 Crore from Rs.39.42 Crore as capital expenditure on the new facility peaked, and cash from financing 
activities was Rs.35.2 Crore against Rs7.47 Crore. Net cash flow for the year was positive at Rs.3.9 Crore and 
the closing cash and bank balance improved to Rs.9.7 Crore from Rs.5.8 Crore. The Company therefore funded 
the largest capital programme in its history while remaining cash flow positive at the net level.

The Company’s key financial ratios for FY2026 reflect the impact of a planned capital investment cycle. The 
debtors turnover ratio improved 47% to 3.95 times, driven by stronger collections even as revenue grew 22%. 
Inventory turnover moderated 33% to 2.89 times, reflecting strategic inventory build to support an enlarged 
order book. The debt-equity ratio rose 110% to 0.42 times following term borrowing drawdowns for capital 
expenditure, while the DSCR declined 71.28% to 2.48 times. 
8. Outlook
The Company enters FY2026-27 with an order book of Rs.588.17 Crore and installed capacity that is substantially 
under-utilised. At the FY2025-26 earnings conference call, management guided to consolidated revenue of 
Rs.275-300 Crore for FY2026-27, with a further step-up expected in FY2027-28, and to EBITDA margins being 
maintained in the range of 15-18%, with the slightly better realisation available on larger transformers offset 
by the higher overhead and skilled manpower costs those units carry.
Three factors will determine whether that guidance is met. The first is ramp-up at Kannur: the plant is expected 
to reach approximately 90% utilisation over two to three years, and the pace of that curve is the single largest 
variable in the medium-term revenue picture. The second is qualification in the higher-rated segment, where 
each new voltage class and customer requires type testing and approval before repeat business follows. The 
third is raw material availability and pricing, particularly CRGO steel, where, on the Global Trade Research 
Initiative’s assessment, the industry remains import-dependent. Against these, the demand environment is 
as supportive as it has been at any point in the Company’s history, and the multi-year nature of the national 
transmission programme means that the opportunity is not contingent on any single year’s ordering.
Over the longer term, the Board believes the combination of expanded capacity, a widened product range, a 
diversified customer base and improved credit standing positions the Company to participate meaningfully 
in India’s power infrastructure build-out while creating sustainable value for shareholders.

10. Internal Control Systems and Their Adequacy
The Company maintains internal control systems commensurate with the nature, size and complexity of 
its operations. These systems are designed to provide reasonable assurance regarding the safeguarding 
of assets against unauthorised use or disposal, the accuracy and completeness of accounting records, 
compliance with applicable statutes and internal policies, and the reliability of financial reporting. Standard 
operating procedures cover procurement, production, quality assurance, dispatch, sales and collection, with 
defined authority levels for approval.
The internal audit function reviews the adequacy and effectiveness of these controls on a periodic basis. 
Internal audit observations, together with management responses and the status of corrective actions, are 
reported to the Audit Committee of the Board, which reviews them and monitors implementation. Per the 
outcome of the Board meeting dated 27 May, 2026 filed with the National Stock Exchange, the Board has 
appointed M/s. Jeevan & Associates as internal auditor and M/s. N. Sivashankaran & Co. as cost auditor for 
FY2026-27. With the commissioning of the second manufacturing facility, the control framework has been 
extended to cover the additional location, including inventory controls, plant-level authorisation limits and 
fixed asset records.
11. Human Resources and Industrial Relations
The Company regards its people as central to its ability to deliver engineered products to specification and 
on schedule. Employee costs increased to Rs.4.5 Crore in FY2025-26 from Rs.3.3 Crore, reflecting recruitment 
for the new manufacturing facility across design, production, testing and quality functions. The higher-rated 
transformer segment requires a deeper pool of skilled and experienced technical personnel, and attracting 
and retaining that talent - in a market where demand for transformer design and testing skills is rising 
across the industry - is an operational priority and a recognised cost pressure.
The Company invests in structured on-the-job training, particularly in coil winding, core assembly, tanking 
and testing, where quality outcomes depend directly on skill. Occupational health and safety is managed 
under an ISO 45001:2018 certified framework. Industrial relations remained cordial throughout the year. The 
Board records its appreciation of the commitment shown by employees at both facilities during a year in 
which a new plant was commissioned alongside the execution of a growing order book.
12. Environment, Health and Safety
Environmental management is certified to ISO 14001:2015 and occupational health and safety to ISO 
45001:2018. The Company’s products themselves contribute to environmental outcomes: energy efficient 
transformers reduce technical losses across distribution networks, and solar, windmill and inverter duty 
transformers enable the evacuation and integration of renewable generation. Manufacturing operations 
focus on responsible handling and disposal of transformer oil and process waste, controlled consumption 
of energy and water, and safe working practices supported by training, protective equipment and periodic 
audit. The Company also undertakes corporate social responsibility activities in the communities around its 
facilities, as detailed elsewhere in this Annual Report. Disclosure of Accounting Treatment: 
Disclosure of Accounting Treatment:
The financial statements of the Company have been prepared in accordance with the applicable Accounting 
Standards (AS) and the provisions of the Companies Act, 2013. The Company has not followed any accounting 
treatment different from that prescribed under the applicable Accounting Standards during the financial 
year 2025-26.

21. Members may please note that SEBI vide its Circular No. SEBI/ HO/MIRSD/MIRSD_RTAMB/P/CIR/2022/8 dated 25 
January, 2022 has mandated the listed companies to issue securities in dematerialised form only while processing 
service requests viz. issue of duplicate securities certificate; claim from an unclaimed suspense account; renewal/
exchange of securities certificate; endorsement; sub-division/splitting of securities certificate; consolidation of 
securities certificates/ folios; transmission and transposition. Accordingly, Members are requested to make service 
requests by submitting a duly filled and signed Form ISR-4 for the above-mentioned requests and surrender their 
original securities certificate(s)for processing of service requests to the RTA. The RTA shall thereafter issue a ‘Letter of 
confirmation’ in lieu of physical securities certificate(s), to the securities holder/claimant within 30 days of its receipt 
of such request after removing objections, if any. The ‘Letter of Confirmation’ shall be valid for a period of 120 days from 
the date of its issuance, within which the securities holder/claimant shall make a request to the DP for dematerialising 
the said securities. Form ISR-4 is available on the website of RTA. It may be noted that any service request can be 
processed only after the folio is KYC Compliant.

Step 1: Access to NSDL e-Voting system
A)  Login method for e-Voting and joining virtual meeting for Individual shareholders holding securities in demat 
mode
 In terms of SEBI circular dated 9 December, 2020 on e-Voting facility provided by Listed Companies, Individual 
shareholders holding securities in demat mode are allowed to vote through their demat account maintained with 
Depositories and Depository Participants. Shareholders are advised to update their mobile number and email Id in their 
demat accounts in order to access e-Voting facility.

5. Password details for shareholders other than Individual shareholders are given below: 
a) If you are already registered for e-Voting, then you can user your existing password to login and cast your vote.
b) If you are using NSDL e-Voting system for the first time, you will need to retrieve the ‘initial password’ which

and the system will force you to change your password.
c) How to retrieve your ‘initial password’?
(i) If your email ID is registered in your demat account or with the company, your ‘initial password’ is 
communicated to you on your email ID. Trace the email sent to you from NSDL from your mailbox. Open

is your 8-digit client ID for NSDL account, last 8 digits of client ID for CDSL account or folio number for

15 minutes after such scheduled time and will be available on first come first served basis.

Item No.6: Approval of related party transactions between the Company and Danya Electric Company (Partnership 
firm).
In furtherance of its business activities, the Company have entered into / will enter into transactions / contract(s) / 
agreement(s) / arrangement(s) with related parties in terms of Regulation 2(1)(zc)(i) of the Securities and Exchange Board 
of India (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015 (“Listing Regulations”). All related party 
transactions of the Company are at arm’s length and in the ordinary course of business. The Company enters into transactions 
with Danya Electric Company, a Partnership firm in which the company holds 90% of share of profits the remaining being 
held by Mr. Vee Rajmohan and Mr. K V Pradeep Director of the Company.
In term of Regulation 23 of SEBI Listing Regulations as amended, any transactions with a related party shall be considered 
material, if the transaction(s) entered into/ to be entered into individually or taken together with the previous transactions 
during a financial year exceeds Rs.50 crore or 10% of annual consolidated turnover of the Company as per the last audited 
financial statements of the Company, whichever is lower, and shall require prior approval of shareholders by means of an 
ordinary resolution. The said limits are applicable, even if the transactions are in the ordinary course of business of the 
concerned company and at an arm’s length basis. 
Danya Electric Company, is primarily engaged in the business of production, manufacturing of Transformers and repair 
and recondition of the same. The company has a business relationship with the said firm for a number of years wherein the 
business activities of the said firm supplements both the business and revenue of the company. 
Further, all related party transactions of the Company are undertaken after obtaining prior approval of the Audit Committee. All 
related party transactions as set out in this Notice have been unanimously approved by the Audit Committee after satisfying 
itself that the related party transactions are at arm’s length and in the ordinary course of business. The Audit Committee of 
the Company reviews periodically the details of all related party transactions entered pursuant to its approvals. 
The value of transactions (for which the approval is being sought) for the period commencing from 1 April, 2026 till the date 
of this Notice has not exceeded the materiality threshold and is not likely to exceed the materiality threshold till the approval 
of these transactions by the members.
The Audit Committee of the Company has, on the basis of relevant details provided by the management as required by the 
law, at its meeting held on February 09, 2026 reviewed and approved the said transaction(s), subject to approval of the 
Members, while noting that such transaction shall be on arms’ length basis and in the ordinary course of business of the 
Company and are in accordance with Related Party Transactions Policy of the Company.

6. Justification as to why the RPTs proposed to be 
entered into are in the interest of the listed entity.
The Company has been maintaining a long-standing 
business relationship with the Firm, which forms an 
integral part of its overall business strategy and risk 
management framework. Although the Company and 
the Firm operate in the same line of business, the 
nature of operations and associated industry factors 
make such collaboration commercially prudent and 
strategically beneficial.
The present business model with the Firm enables the 
Company to:
• Maximize Revenues: By leveraging complementary 
strengths and market opportunities, the Company 
is able to enhance its revenue generation potential.
• Optimize Costs: Shared resources, infrastructure 
support, and operational coordination help achieve 
significant cost savings and efficiency gains.
• Realize Synergies: The collaboration allows the 
Company to capitalize on business synergies 
that would otherwise require significant time and 
investment to develop independently.
• Enhance Risk Mitigation: The continuity of 
business with an established counterparty 
provides operational stability and reduces supply-
side and execution risks.
Accordingly, the proposed transactions are considered 
to be in the ordinary course of business, conducted 
on an arm’s length basis, and are in the overall best 
interest of the Company and its public shareholders. 
These arrangements are expected to contribute 
positively towards the Company’s long-term growth, 
competitiveness, and value creation.
The Audit Committee and the Board of Directors, after 
due review and consideration, have approved the 
proposed Related Party Transactions, and are of the 
opinion that the same are fair, reasonable, and in the 
best interests of the Company and its shareholders.

ITEM NO. 9: Approval for giving loan or guarantee or providing security under Section 185 of the Companies Act, 2013 
Pursuant to the provisions of Section 185 of the Companies Act, 2013 (“the Act”), read with the Companies (Meetings of 
Board and its Powers) Rules, 2014 (as amended from time to time), no company shall, directly or indirectly, advance any loan 
(including any loan represented by a book debt), or give any guarantee, or provide any security in connection with any loan 
taken by (a) any Director of the Company, or of its holding company, or any partner or relative of any such Director; or (b) any 
firm in which such Director or relative is a partner.
However, as per Section 185(2) of the Act, a company may advance any loan (including any loan represented by a book debt), 
or give any guarantee, or provide any security in connection with any loan taken by any person in whom any Director of the 
Company is interested, subject to the following conditions:
• the company passes a special resolution in the general meeting; and
• such loans/guarantees/securities are utilized by the borrowing entity for its principal business activities.
In this regard, it is submitted that, the Company, in the ordinary course of business and pursuant to the stipulations of 
IndusInd Bank Limited (“the Lender”), has extended Corporate Guarantee(s) aggregating to Rs. 14.70 Crores on behalf of 
Danya Electric Company (“the Firm”), in respect of the credit facilities sanctioned by the Lender. The Firm is a partnership 
entity in which the Company holds 90% profit-sharing interest, while the balance 10% is held by Mr. Vee Rajmohan (DIN: 
00844400), Chairman & Managing Director, and Mr. Vishwambran Nair Pradeep Kumar (DIN: 10218276), Whole-time Director, 
who are also Promoter-Directors of the Company. In addition, the said Promoter-Directors have also extended their personal 
guarantees to the Lender in respect of the aforesaid facilities.
Considering the substantial stake of the Company in the Firm and in order to facilitate its business operations, it is proposed 
to authorise the Board of Directors to continue to provide, from time to time, Corporate Guarantee(s) and/or Security(ies) in 
favour of lenders, in respect of the credit facilities availed or to be availed by the Firm. The aforesaid approval shall also be 
deemed to cover the Corporate Guarantee(s) already extended by the Company on behalf of the Firm.
Further, in order to enable the Company to provide loan(s), including loan(s) represented by way of book debt, and/or give 
guarantee(s), and/or provide security(ies) in connection with loan(s) taken or to be taken by any of the group entities, or 
any other person in whom any of the Directors of the Company may be deemed to be interested (as per Explanation to Sec-
tion 185(2)(b) of the Act), it is proposed to seek the approval of shareholders by way of Special Resolution, for an aggregate 
amount not exceeding Rs.75,00,00,000/- (Rupees Seventy Five Crores only).
The said limit of Rs. 75 Crores shall be inclusive of the Corporate Guarantee(s) already extended by the Company on behalf 
of Danya Electric Company; and the guarantees/securities/loans proposed to be extended to the Firm or any such person/
entity covered under Section 185(2)(b) of the Act.
The aforesaid loan(s), guarantee(s), and/or security(ies) shall be utilized strictly for the principal business activities of the 
borrowing entity and in the best interests of the Company.
Accordingly, the Board recommends the resolution set out at Item No. 9 of the Notice for approval of the Members by way of 
Special Resolution.
Except Mr. Vee Rajmohan (DIN: 00844400), Chairman & Managing Director, and Mr. Vishwambran Nair Pradeep Kumar (DIN: 
10218276), Whole-time Director, and their respective relatives, none of the other Directors, Key Managerial Personnel of the 
Company, and/or their relatives are concerned or interested, financially or otherwise, in the proposed resolution.

ITEM NO. 7: Approval of the overall Borrowing Limits u/s 180(1)(c) of the Companies Act, 2013 
Keeping in view the Company’s long term strategic and business objectives, the Company may need additional funds. For 
this purpose, the Company may, from time to time, raise finance from various Banks and/or Financial Institutions and/
or any other lending institutions and/or Bodies Corporate and/or such other persons/ individuals as may be considered 
fit, which, together with the moneys already borrowed by the Company (apart from temporary loans obtained from the 
Company’s bankers in ordinary course of business) may exceed the aggregate of the paid-up capital and free reserves of the 
Company. Pursuant to section 180(1)(c) and other applicable provisions of companies act, 2013 provides that the Board of 
Directors shall not borrow in excess of the Company’s paid up share capital and free reserves, apart from temporary loans 
obtained from the Company’s bankers in the ordinary course of business, except with the consent of the Company accorded 
by way of a Special Resolution.
Accordingly, the members of the company previously approved an amount of up to Rs.300 crores as the borrowing limit 
under Section 180(1)(c) of the Act.  Considering the increase in the business of the company and the expansion plans it is 
proposed to increase the limit of borrowing under Section 180(1)(c) of the Act to Rs. 500 Crore/- (Rupees Five Hundred Crores) 
to enable the Board to borrow money depending on the requirements of the company from time to time.
The above proposal is in the interest of the Company and the Board recommends the Resolution as set out at Item no. 7 for 
approval by the members of the Company as Special Resolutions.
None of the Directors or Key Managerial Personnel or their relatives are in any way concerned with or interested, financially 
or otherwise in the said resolution except to the extent of their shareholding in the Company, if any.
ITEM NO. 8: Creation of mortgage or charge on the assets, properties or undertaking(s) of the Company under Section 
180(1)(a) of the Companies Act, 2013 
In order to secure the borrowing made by the Company by the lenders, it would be necessary to create charge on the assets or 
whole or part of the undertaking of the Company in favour of the lenders. In terms of the provisions of Section 180(1)(a) read 
with Section 110 of the Companies Act, 2013 and Rules made thereunder consent of the Shareholders of the Company by way 
of a Special Resolution through Postal Ballot is required to sell, lease or otherwise dispose off the whole or substantially the 
whole of the undertaking or undertakings of the company. 
In terms of MCA Notification dated 9 February, 2018, any item of business required to be transacted by means of postal 
ballot, may be transacted at a general meeting by a company which is required to provide the facility to members to vote 
by electronic means.an terms of the provisions of Companies Act, 2013 and SEBI (LODR) Regulations, 2015, the company is 
providing the facility to its members to enable them to vote on resolutions at the general meeting, by electronic means. 
Accordingly, the Special Resolution for creation of mortgage and/or charge on all or any of the movable and/or immovable 
properties of the Company, is proposed to be passed in this AGM. 
The Company has obtained approval of the members under Section 180(1)(a) of the Companies Act, 2013 for an amount of up 
to Rs.300 crores. In view of the increase in the borrowing limit under Section 180(1)(c) of the Act and the requirements under 
Section 180(1)(a) of the Act, approval of the members is sought by way of a Special Resolution under Section 180(1)(a) of the 
Companies Act, 2013 for creation of security on the assets of the company up to an amount of Rs. 500 crore/- (Rupees Five 
Hundred Crores Only) if and when necessary. 
The above proposal is in the interest of the Company and the Board recommends the Resolution as set out at Item no. 8 for 
approval by the members of the Company as Special Resolutions.

COMPANY PERFORMANCE OVERVIEW:
During the financial year 2025-26, total revenue from operations on a standalone basis stood at Rs.190.07 crores as against 
Rs. 144.79 crores in the previous year 2024-25 registering a growth of 31.27 %. The total expenditure of the company was Rs. 
165.49 Crores (on Standalone basis) as against Rs. 124.39 Crores in the previous year 2024-25, an increase of 33.04 %. Profit 
before exceptional items and tax for the current year is Rs. 26.91 crores as against Rs. 23.95 crores in the previous year 2024-
25 a growth of 12.35%. Profit after tax for the current year is Rs. 20.44 crores as against Rs. 18.60 crores in the previous year 
2024-25 a growth of 9.89%. 
During the financial year 2025-26, total revenue from operations on a consolidation basis was Rs. 181.64 crores as against 
Rs. 148.72 crores in the previous year 2024-25 registering a growth of 22.13%. The total expenditure of the company was Rs. 
153.58 Crores (on Consolidation basis) as against Rs. 123.48 Crores in the previous year 2024-25, an increase of 24.37 %. 
Profit before exceptional items and tax for the current year is Rs. 28.51 crores as against Rs. 26.05 crores in the previous year 
2024-25 a growth of 9.44 %. Profit after tax for the current year is Rs.20.67 crores as against Rs. 18.92 crores in the previous 
year 2024-25 a growth of 9.24%. 
As the company’s business primarily falls within a single business segment, there is no disclosure to be provided under 
Accounting Standard 17, “Segment Reporting. There has been no change in the legal status of the Company during the year 
under review. The Company continues to operate in the same class and category as in the previous year. The financial year of 
the Company remains unchanged. The Company continues to follow the financial year commencing on 1st April and ending 
on 31st March, in accordance with the provisions of the Companies Act, 2013.
No acquisition, merger, activities were undertaken during the year under review. The operations of the Company continued 
in the normal course of business without any structural or strategic changes. The Company has not acquired, assigned, or 
developed any material Intellectual Property Rights during the year under review.

DIVIDEND
The Board considering the need to conserve capital to fund the expansion activities as well as to augment working capital 
requirements dhas decided not to recommend any dividend for the financial year ended March 31, 2026.
To bring transparency in the matter of declaration of dividend and protect the interests of investors, the company had 
adopted a Dividend Distribution Policy since listing of its shares. The policy has been displayed on the Company’s website 
at link https://www.supremepower.in/policy-Files/P11_Dividend%20Distribution%20Policy.pdf
UNCLAIMED DIVIDEND
In the absence of any declaration of dividend in the past, the Company does not have any unpaid/unclaimed dividend 
coming under the purview of Section 124(5) of the Act to be transferred to Investors Education and Protection Fund (“IEPF”) 
of the Central Government.
TRANSFER TO RESERVES
During the year under review, no amount was transferred to any of the reserves by the Company. The Board has decided to 
retain the entire amount of profit for FY26 in the distributable retained earnings.
QUALITY ASSURANCE 
Your Company poses ISO 9001:2015, ISO 14001:2015 and ISO 45001:2018 certificates in the area of Design, Manufacture, 
Services and Supply of Transformers. The Company has its Quality Management System certified by TUV/QACS. CPRI (“Central 
Power Research Institute”) has type tested our transformers upto 25MVA/110kV Voltage Class. Right from manufacturing 
to distribution, the company has consistently delivered competitive edge in the form of robust, foresighted, and quality 
products.
AWARDS, RECOGNITION AND CERTIFICATIONS 
The company has obtained various certifications which includes ISO Certificate 45001:2018, 14001:2015, 9001:2015 and BIS 
6700052811 which stands testimony for the highest standards of quality and safety maintained by the Company in respect 
of its products.
The company has been awarded the MSME Ratna Award for Electronics Innovation and Engineering Excellence on December 
22, 2025 by Chamber of Indian Micro Small & Medium Enterprises, this recognition is a testament to our company’s 
commitment to excellence and innovation in the MSME sector. It highlights our dedication to quality, customer satisfaction, 
and our strategic initiatives aimed at driving growth and strengthening our market position. Disclosure of MSME Ratna 
Award is available at Company’s website at https://www.supremepower.in/IPO_pdf/UpdateMSMEaward.pdf

Board’s Report
BUSINESS OUTLOOK
The Company is mainly engaged in the business of manufacturing, up-gradation, and refurbishment of transformers ranging 
from Power Transformer, Generator Transformer, Windmill Transformer, Distribution Transformer, Isolation Transformer, Solar 
Transformer, Energy Efficient Transformer, Converter and Rectifier Transformer. 
Considering the continued increase in expenditure on infrastructure development along with the positive trajectory of the 
overall economy, the Company anticipates a significant expansion in its order book. This anticipated growth is expected to 
strengthen the Company’s business position and will be reflected in higher revenues as well as improved profitability in the 
forthcoming periods
A more detailed explanation of the business and the performance of the Company has been provided in the Management 
Discussion and Analysis Report, which is forming part of the Annual Report.
CHANGE IN THE NATURE BUSINESS OF COMPANY
During the year under review, there was no change in the nature of the Company’s business.
LISTING OF THE COMPANY
The Shares of your Company are listed on Platform of “Emerge Platform on National Stock Exchange”. The annual Listing Fees 
for the Year 2026-27 has been paid to the exchange.
